Legislative powers are exercised by a 300-member [unicameral Parliament](204304.md).[^cite_note-con51,53-184] According to the Constitution, executive power is exercised by the [Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Greece) and the [President of the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Greece), who is the nominal head of state, is elected by the [Parliament](2449826.md) for a five-year term and promulgates statutes passed by Parliament.[^cite_note-con51,53-184] However, the [Constitutional amendment of 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Constitutional_amendment_of_1986) rendered the President's office largely ceremonial; the most powerful officeholder is thus the [prime minister](273647.md), Greece's head of government.[^cite_note-M477-478-187] The position is filled by the [current leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Prime_Ministers_of_Greece) of the [political party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Greece) that can obtain a vote of confidence by the Parliament. The president of the republic formally appoints the prime minister and, on their recommendation, appoints and dismisses the other members of the Cabinet.[^cite_note-con51,53-184]&#x20;



The building of the [Hellenic Parliament](2449826.md) ([Old Royal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Royal_Palace)) in central [Athens](1216.md)

Members of Parliament are elected in [direct elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_elections), which [are conducted with a system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apportionment_in_the_Hellenic_Parliament) of [semi-proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-proportional_representation) with a [majority bonus system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_bonus_system), usually leading to the formation of single-party governments.[^cite_note-188] [Parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Greece#Election_of_the_legislature) are held every four years, but early elections are proclaimed by the President on the cabinet's proposal or if a [motion of no confidence](208101.md) passes in Parliament.[^cite_note-con51,53-184] The [voting age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_age) is 17.[^cite_note-189] [Women's suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage) was legislated in [1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Constitution_of_1952).&#x20;

According to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance)'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Greece performs in the mid-range on overall democratic measures, with particular strengths in elected government and freedom of movement.[^cite_note-190] Moreover, according to an OECD report, Greeks display a moderate level of civic participation compared to most other developed countries; voter turnout was 58% during recent elections, lower than the OECD average of 69%.[^cite_note-191]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Administrative divisions of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Greece) and [Regions of Greece](221397.md)

Since the [Kallikratis Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kallikratis_Programme) reform entered into effect in January 2011, Greece has consisted of 13 [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_regions_of_Greece), subdivided into [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Greece), of which there are now 332 (there were 325 before the [Kleisthenis I Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleisthenis_I_Programme) came into effect in 2019). The 54 old [prefectures and prefecture-level administrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefectures_of_Greece) have been largely retained as *[sub-units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_units_of_Greece)* of the regions. Seven [decentralised administrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decentralized_administrations_of_Greece) group one to three regions for administrative purposes on a regional basis. There is one [autonomous area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_area), [Mount Athos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastic_community_of_Mount_Athos) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): *Agio Oros*, "Holy Mountain"),[^cite_note-192] which borders the region of [Central Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Macedonia).[^cite_note-193]&#x20;

### Political parties

Main article: [List of political parties in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Greece)

After the [restoration of democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metapolitefsi) in 1974–1975, the Greek party system was dominated by the liberal-conservative [New Democracy](424225.md) (ND) and the social-democratic [Panhellenic Socialist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PASOK) (PASOK).[^cite_note-196] PASOK and New Democracy largely alternated in power until the outbreak of the [government-debt crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_government-debt_crisis) in 2009, whenceforth they experienced a sharp decline in popularity,[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] manifested in the [parliamentary elections of May 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_legislative_election,_May_2012), when the left-wing [SYRIZA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SYRIZA) became the second major party,[^cite_note-202] overtaking PASOK as the main party of the centre-left.[^cite_note-203] After a [repeat election in June 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2023_Greek_legislative_election), New Democracy gained almost 41% of the popular vote and a parliamentary majority of 158 MPs and its leader, [Kyriakos Mitsotakis](2780816.md), who [had been Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Cabinet_of_Kyriakos_Mitsotakis) from [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Greek_legislative_election) until the [inconclusive election of May 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2023_Greek_legislative_election), was sworn in for a [second four-year term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Cabinet_of_Kyriakos_Mitsotakis).[^cite_note-204] Other parties represented in the [Hellenic Parliament](2449826.md) are the [Communist Party of Greece](356444.md) (KKE), [Greek Solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Solution), [New Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Left_\(Greece\)), [Spartans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spartans_\(Greek_political_party\)), [Victory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_\(Greek_political_party\)) and [Course of Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Course_of_Freedom).&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Greece)



Representation through:

[^cite_note-205]

\
&#x20; embassy\
&#x20; embassy in another country\
&#x20; general consulate\
&#x20; no representation\
&#x20; Greece

Foreign policy is conducted through the [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Greece\)) and its head, the [Minister for Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Foreign_Affairs_\(Greece\)), currently [Giorgos Gerapetritis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgos_Gerapetritis). The aims of the Ministry are to represent Greece before other states and international organisations; safeguard the interests of the state and its citizens abroad; promote Greek culture; foster closer relations with the [Greek diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora); and encourage international cooperation.[^cite_note-MFA_mission-206] Greece is described as having a special relationship with [Cyprus](5593.md), Italy, France, Armenia, Australia, Israel, the US and the UK.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]&#x20;

Following the resolution of the [Macedonia naming dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_naming_dispute) with the [Prespa Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prespa_Agreement) in 2018, the Ministry identifies two remaining issues of particular importance to the Greek state: [Turkish challenges to Greek sovereignty rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_dispute) in the Aegean Sea and corresponding airspace, and the [Cyprus problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprus_problem) involving the [Turkish occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_military_forces_in_Northern_Cyprus) of [Northern Cyprus](271409.md).[^cite_note-MFA_issues-213] There is a long-standing conflict between Turkey and Greece over natural resources in the eastern Mediterranean. Turkey does not recognise a legal [continental shelf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_shelf) and [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) around the Greek islands.[^cite_note-214]&#x20;

Due to its geographical proximity to Europe, Asia, the Middle East and Africa, Greece is of geostrategic importance, which it has leveraged to develop a regional policy to promote peace and stability in the [Balkans](4829.md), Mediterranean and the Middle East.[^cite_note-MFA_regional_policy-215] This has accorded the country [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) status.[^cite_note-Thanos-Veremēs-216]&#x20;

Greece is a member of numerous international organisations, including the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe), the [European Union](9317.md), the [Union for the Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_the_Mediterranean), [NATO](21133.md), the [Organisation internationale de la francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_francophonie) and the UN, of which it is a founding member.&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [Hellenic Armed Forces](12116.md)



An [F-16 Fighting Falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-16_Fighting_Falcon), the main combat aircraft of the [Hellenic Air Force](363254.md), during an airshow



A [Leopard 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopard_2)A6 HEL of the [Hellenic Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Army) on parade in Athens

The Hellenic Armed Forces are overseen by the [Hellenic National Defence General Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_National_Defence_General_Staff) (Greek: Γενικό Επιτελείο Εθνικής Άμυνας – ΓΕΕΘΑ), with civilian authority vested in the [Ministry of National Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Defence_\(Greece\)). It consists of three branches:[^cite_note-217] the [Hellenic Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Army) (Ellinikos Stratos, ES), the [Hellenic Navy](425896.md) (Elliniko Polemiko Navtiko, EPN) and the [Hellenic Air Force](363254.md) (Elliniki Polemiki Aeroporia, EPA).&#x20;

Moreover, Greece maintains the [Hellenic Coast Guard](2151356.md) for law enforcement at sea, search and rescue, and port operations. Though it can support the navy during wartime, it resides under the authority of the [Ministry of Shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Shipping_\(Greece\)).&#x20;

Greek military personnel total 364,050, of whom 142,700 are active and 221,350 are reserve. Greece [ranks 28th in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_military_and_paramilitary_personnel) in the number of citizens serving in the armed forces. [Mandatory military service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Greece) is generally one year for 19 to 45 year olds.[^cite_note-Factbook-Greece-161] Additionally, Greek males between the ages of 18 and 60 who live in strategically sensitive areas may be required to serve part-time in the National Guard.&#x20;

As a member of [NATO](21133.md), the Greek military participates in exercises and deployments under the auspices of the alliance, although its involvement in NATO missions is minimal.[^cite_note-218] Greece spends over US\$7 billion annually on its military, or 2.3% of GDP, the [24th-highest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_military_expenditures) in absolute terms, the [seventh-highest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_military_expenditure_per_capita) on a per capita basis, and the second-highest in NATO after the United States. Moreover, Greece is one of only five NATO countries to meet or surpass the minimum defence spending target of 2% of GDP.&#x20;

### Law and justice

Main articles: [Judiciary of Greece](4544481.md) and [Hellenic Police](10063533.md)

The judiciary is independent of the executive and the legislature and comprises three Supreme Courts: the [Supreme Civil and Criminal Court of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Civil_and_Criminal_Court_of_Greece), the [Council of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_State_\(Greece\)) and the [Court of Audit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Audit_\(Greece\)). The judicial system is also composed of civil courts, which judge civil and penal cases and administrative courts, which judge disputes between citizens and the Greek administrative authorities.&#x20;

The Hellenic Police is the national police force. It is a large agency with its responsibilities ranging from [road traffic control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_traffic_control) to [counter-terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-terrorism). It was established in 1984, after the merge of the [Hellenic Gendarmerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Gendarmerie) and the [Cities Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_Police) forces.[^cite_note-219]&#x20;

## Economy

Main articles: [Economy of Greece](12113.md) and [List of Greek subdivisions by GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Greek_subdivisions_by_GDP)



GDP per capita development

As of 2025, the economy was the [54th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) largest by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) (PPP) at \$467.590 billion. In per person income, Greece is [47th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita) in the world at \$45,048.[^cite_note-220] Greece is the 15th largest economy in the 27‑member [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-221] The economy is advanced[^cite_note-Advanced_economies-222][^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224][^cite_note-High_income_economies-225][^cite_note-226] and [high-income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_income_economy).[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-High_income_economies-225]&#x20;

Greece is a [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) with a high [standard of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living) and high ranking in the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index).[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230] Its economy mainly comprises the [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_sector) (85%) and [industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_sector) (12%), while agriculture makes up 3%.[^cite_note-GDP_by_sector-231] Important Greek industries include [tourism](159622.md) (with 33 million[^cite_note-232] international tourists in 2023, it is the [9th most visited country in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_rankings)) and [merchant shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Merchant_Navy) (at 18% of the world's total capacity,[^cite_note-auto-233] the Greek merchant marine is the largest in the world), while the country is a considerable agricultural producer (including fisheries) within the union. In 2021 unemployment stood at 13% and [youth unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_unemployment) at 33%, compared with respectively 7% and 16% in the EU and eurozone.[^cite_note-234]&#x20;

Greece has the largest economy in the Balkans,[^cite_note-BalkanInsight-235][^cite_note-Keridis-236][^cite_note-237] and an important regional investor.[^cite_note-BalkanInsight-235][^cite_note-Keridis-236] It has been the number-two foreign investor of capital in Albania and most important trading partner and largest foreign investor in North Macedonia.[^cite_note-AydinIfantis2004-238][^cite_note-Thompson2012-239] The Greek telecommunications company [OTE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OTE) has become a strong investor in other Balkan countries.[^cite_note-Bell2002-240]&#x20;

Greece was a founding member of the [OECD](33853117.md) and the [Organisation of the Black Sea Economic Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_the_Black_Sea_Economic_Cooperation) (BSEC). In 1979, its accession to the [European Communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Communities) and the [single market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Market_\(European_Union\)) was signed, and completed in 1982. Greece was accepted into the [Economic and Monetary Union of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_and_Monetary_Union_of_the_European_Union) in June 2000, and in January 2001 adopted the euro as its currency, replacing the [Greek drachma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_drachma).[^cite_note-Drachma_exchange_rate-241] Greece is a member of the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) and the [World Trade Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization).&#x20;

### Debt crisis (2010–2018)

Main article: [Greek government-debt crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_government-debt_crisis)



Greece's debt percentage since 1977, compared to the average of the [eurozone](184391.md)

Greek economy had fared well (with high growth rates and low public debt) during most of the 20th century; high growth rates were maintained up to the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), coupled, however, with high structural [deficits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_budget_balance).[^cite_note-Debt_Past-242] In 2009, it was revealed deficits had been considerably higher than official figures.[^cite_note-\:3-243] Banks had supplied cash in exchange for future payments by Greece and other Eurozone countries; in turn the liabilities of the countries were "kept off the books", hiding borrowing levels.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245][^cite_note-The_Guardian-246] This was one of the techniques that enabled Greece to reduce its recorded budget deficit.[^cite_note-Der_Spiegel-247]&#x20;

The crisis was triggered by the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), which caused Greece's GDP to contract 2.5% in 2009.[^cite_note-248] Simultaneously, deficits were revealed to have been allowed to reach 10% and 15% in 2008 and 2009. This caused Greece's debt-to-GDP ratio to increase to 127%.[^cite_note-249] As a [eurozone](184391.md) member, Greece had no autonomous [monetary policy flexibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_and_Monetary_Union_of_the_European_Union#Monetary_policy_inflexibility). Greece's borrowing rates increased, causing a crisis of confidence in Greece's ability to pay back loans in early 2010.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251]&#x20;

To avert a [sovereign default](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_default), Greece, other eurozone members, and the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) agreed on a €110 billion rescue package in May 2010.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253] Greece was required to adopt harsh [austerity](684037.md) measures to bring its deficit down.[^cite_note-254] A second bail-out of €130 billion was agreed in 2012, subject to financial reforms and further austerity.[^cite_note-BBCQ\&A-255] A [debt haircut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt_relief) was agreed.[^cite_note-BBCQ\&A-255] Greece achieved a [budget surplus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balanced_budget) in 2013 and returned to growth in 2014.[^cite_note-Kathimerini_growth-256][^cite_note-257]&#x20;

Partly due to the imposed austerity,[^cite_note-\:3-243] Greece experienced a 25% drop in GDP between 2009 and 2015.[^cite_note-258] The debt ratio, jumped from 127% to about 170%, due to the shrinking economy.[^cite_note-Debt_2017_Eurostat-259] In 2013, the IMF admitted it had underestimated the effects of tax hikes and budget cuts and issued an informal apology.[^cite_note-IMF_Guardian-260][^cite_note-IMF_Reuters-261][^cite_note-Fiscal_adjustment-262] The policies have been blamed for worsening the crisis,[^cite_note-IMF_Bloomberg-263][^cite_note-IMF_WSJ-264] while others stressed the creditors' share in responsibility.[^cite_note-Pavlopoulos_Moscovici-265][^cite_note-Tsipras_Bloomberg-266][^cite_note-Debt_2017_Eurostat-259] The bailouts ended in 2018.[^cite_note-Bailout_exit_Reuters-155]&#x20;

In 2024, the Greek economy was forecast to grow by nearly 3%, approaching its pre-crisis size of 2009 and far outpacing the eurozone's average economic growth of 0.8%.[^cite_note-267]&#x20;

In March 2026, it was reported that Greece would repay a further €7 billion from its first bailout package ahead of schedule as part of a broader effort to improve its financial standing and reduce debt.[^cite_note-268] This follows previous earlier repayments to EU creditors, as well as completion of repayment of Greece's loans to the International Monetary Fund two years ahead of schedule in 2022.[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] The early debt repayments were seen as part of the country's remarkable recovery following the end of the crisis in 2018.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273]&#x20;

### Industry

Industrial activity was evident in Greece, since at least the second half of the 19th century (including heavy industry such as shipbuilding), mainly centered in [Ermoupolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermoupolis) and [Piraeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraeus).[^cite_note-GrM-274] Manufacturing output increased significantly during the [ period of Greece's rapid economic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_economic_miracle) (1950-1973), followed by a sharp decline after 1980 when services, as in other parts of the world, rapidly replaced manufacturing.[^cite_note-IMF1-275] The value added by the manufacturing sector as a percentage of GDP in Greece has remained lower than in most European countries (see Table).&#x20;

| Country | Value added by manufacturing (% of GDP) |
| --- | --- |
| Germany | 18.36 |
| Italy | 15.26 |
| Spain | 10.89 |
| Netherlands | 10.84 |
| France | 9.73 |
| Greece | 8.73 |
| United Kingdom | 8.28 |

In 2025 Greece's main exports were refined petroleum products (26.2%), food products (18.6%), manufactured goods (15.5%), chemicals (13%), and machinery and transport equipment (9.9%). [^cite_note-GREx-277]&#x20;

### Agriculture

Main article: [Agriculture in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Greece)



Sun-drying of [Zante currant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zante_currant) on [Zakynthos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakynthos)

Greece is the European Union's largest producer of [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton)[^cite_note-278] and [pistachios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pistachios) (7,200 tons in 2021),[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-faocrops-280] second in [olives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive) (3m tons in 2021), third in [figs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fig) (8,400 tons in 2022) and [watermelons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermelon) (440,000 tons in 2022) and fourth in [almonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almond) (40,000 tons in 2022).[^cite_note-faocrops-280] Agriculture contributes 3.8% of GDP and employs 12% of the labour force.&#x20;

Greece is a major beneficiary of the EU's [Common Agricultural Policy](141924.md). As a result of entry to the European Community, much of its agricultural infrastructure has been upgraded and output increased.&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Greece)



Electricity generation in Greece - percentage share

Electricity production is dominated by the state-owned [Public Power Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Power_Corporation) (known by its acronym ΔΕΗ, transliterated as DEI), which supplied 75% of electricity in 2021.[^cite_note-281] Some of DEI's output is generated using [lignite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lignite).[^cite_note-DEI_2010-282] [Renewable energy in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_Greece) accounted for 46% of Greece's electricity in 2022,[^cite_note-283] a rise from the 11% in 2011.[^cite_note-Eurostat_renewable_energy-284] [Wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power) accounts for 22%, [solar power](13690575.md) 14%, [hydropower](14073.md) 9%, and natural gas 38%.[^cite_note-285] Independent companies' energy production has increased. Greece does not have any [nuclear power plants](207347.md). During the 2nd Nuclear Energy Summit in Paris, [Kyriakos Mitsotakis](2780816.md) announced that Greece will begin exploring the possibility of using nuclear energy, especially [Small Modular Reactors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_modular_reactor) (SMRs), as part of its future energy mix.[^cite_note-286]&#x20;

### Maritime

Main articles: [Greek shipping](25159995.md) and [List of ports in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_in_Greece)

See also: [Economy of Greece » Maritime industry](12113.md#Maritime_industry)

The shipping industry has been a key element of economic activity since ancient times.[^cite_note-shipping-287] Shipping remains one of the country's most important industries, accounting for 5% of GDP and employing about 160,000 people (4% of the workforce).[^cite_note-nbg-288]&#x20;

The [Greek Merchant Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Merchant_Navy) is the largest in the world at 18% of global capacity.[^cite_note-auto-233] The merchant fleet ranks first in tonnage (384 million [dwt](1346384.md)), 2nd in number of ships (at 4,870),[^cite_note-auto-233] first in [tankers](2506366.md) and dry bulk carriers, fourth in the number of containers, and fifth in other ships.[^cite_note-BTS-289] The number of ships flying a Greek flag (includes non-Greek fleets) is 1,517, or 5% of the world's tonnage (ranked fifth globally). Today's fleet is smaller than an all-time high of 5,000 ships in the late 1970s.[^cite_note-shipping-287] During the 1960s, the Greek fleet nearly doubled, through the investment undertaken by the shipping magnates, [Aristotle Onassis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_Onassis) and [Stavros Niarchos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stavros_Niarchos).[^cite_note-slate-290] The modern Greek maritime industry was formed after World War II when Greek shipping businessmen were able to amass surplus ships sold by the U.S. government through the Ship Sales Act of the 1940s.[^cite_note-slate-290]&#x20;

Greece has a significant shipbuilding and ship maintenance industry. The six shipyards around the port of [Piraeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraeus) are among the largest in Europe.[^cite_note-291] Greece has become a leader in the construction and maintenance of luxury yachts.[^cite_note-292]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Greece](159622.md)



[Santorini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santorini), a popular tourist destination, is ranked as the world's top island in many travel magazines and sites.

[^cite_note-293]

[^cite_note-294]

Tourism has been a key element of the economy and one of the most important sectors, contributing 21% of gross domestic product in 2018.[^cite_note-295] Greece was the 9th most visited country in the world in 2022, hosting 28 million visitors,[^cite_note-May2023-296] an increase from 18 million tourists in 2007.[^cite_note-Eurostat_Tourism-297]&#x20;

Most visitors come from the European continent,[^cite_note-A2001-298] while the most from a single nationality are from the United Kingdom, followed by Germany. The most visited [region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_regions_of_Greece) of Greece is [Central Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Macedonia).[^cite_note-Eurostat_Tourism_table-299]&#x20;

In 2011, [Santorini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santorini) was voted as "The World's Best Island" in *[Travel + Leisure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_%2B_Leisure)*.[^cite_note-Best_Islands-300] Its neighbouring island [Mykonos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mykonos), came in fifth in the European category.[^cite_note-Best_Islands-300] There are [21 UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_sites_in_Greece) in Greece,[^cite_note-Unesco-301] and Greece is ranked 17th in the world in total sites. Eleven further sites are on the tentative list, awaiting nomination.[^cite_note-Unesco-301]&#x20;



Panoramic view of the old [Corfu City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corfu_City), a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md), as seen from the [Old Fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Fortress,_Corfu). The Bay of Garitsa is to the left and the port of [Corfu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corfu) is just visible on the top right. [Spianada Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spianada_Square) is in the foreground.

### Transport

Main articles: [Transport in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Greece) and [Highways in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highways_in_Greece)



The [Rio–Antirrio bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio%E2%80%93Antirrio_bridge) connects mainland Greece to the [Peloponnese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnese)



Map of Greece's motorway network as of 2022. **Black**=Completed routes, **Blue**=Under Construction, **Grey**=Planned routes



A [Blue Star Ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Star_Ferries) vessel and a Greek navy ship.

Since the 1980s, the road and rail network has been modernised. With a total length of about 2,320 km (1,440 mi) as of 2020, Greece's motorway network is the most extensive in [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe) and one of the most advanced in Europe,[^cite_note-302] including the east–west [A2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A2_motorway_\(Greece\)) (Egnatia Odos) in northern Greece, the north–south [A1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_motorway_\(Greece\)) (Athens–Thessaloniki–Evzonoi, AThE) along the mainland's eastern coastline and the [A5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A5_motorway_\(Greece\)) (Ionia Odos) along the western coastline, leading to the [Rio–Antirrio bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio%E2%80%93Antirrio_bridge), the longest suspension cable bridge in Europe (2,250 m (7,382 ft) long), connecting [Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio,_Greece) in the [Peloponnese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnese) with [Antirrio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antirrio) in western Greece. The Athens Metropolitan Area is served by the privately run [Attiki Odos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attiki_Odos) ([A6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A6_motorway_\(Greece\))/[A62](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A62_motorway_\(Greece\))/[A621](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A621_motorway_\(Greece\))/[A64](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A64_motorway_\(Greece\))/[A65](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A65_motorway_\(Greece\))) motorway network and the expanded [Athens Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athens_Metro) system, while the [Thessaloniki Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Metro) was launched in 2024.&#x20;

Railway connections play a smaller role than in many other European countries but have been expanded, with new suburban/[commuter rail](6286.md) connections, served by [Proastiakos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proastiakos) around Athens, Thessaloniki, and Patras. A modern intercity rail connection between Athens and Thessaloniki has been established, while an upgrade to double lines in many parts of the 2,500 km (1,600 mi) network is underway; along with a [new double track, standard gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P.A.Th.E./P.) railway between [Athens](1216.md) and [Patras](470691.md) (replacing the old [metre-gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre-gauge_railway) [Piraeus–Patras railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraeus%E2%80%93Patras_railway)) which is under construction and opening in stages.[^cite_note-303] International railway lines connect Greek cities with the rest of Europe, the Balkans and Turkey.&#x20;

All major islands are served by ferries to the mainland. [Piraeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraeus), the port of Athens, was the third busiest passenger port in Europe as of 2021. 37 million passengers travelled by boat in Greece in 2019, the second-highest in Europe.[^cite_note-304] Greece has 39 active airports, 15 of which serve international destinations.[^cite_note-305] [Athens International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athens_International_Airport) served over 28 million passengers in 2023.[^cite_note-AIA-306] Most Greek islands and main cities are connected by air by the three major airlines: [Olympic Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Air), [Aegean Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Airlines) and [Sky Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_Express_\(Greece\)).&#x20;

### Telecommunications

Main article: [Telecommunications in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Greece)

Modern digital information and communication networks reach all areas. There are over 35,000 km (21,748 mi) of fiber optics and an extensive open-wire network. Broadband internet availability is widespread in Greece: there were a total of 2,252,653 broadband connections as of early 2011, translating to 20% broadband penetration.[^cite_note-cnbc-307] In 2017 around 82% of the population used the internet regularly.[^cite_note-Kathimerini_article-308]&#x20;

[Internet cafés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_caf%C3%A9) that provide net access, office applications and multiplayer gaming are a common sight, while mobile internet on [3G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3G) and [4G](486547.md)- [LTE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LTE_\(telecommunication\)) cellphone networks and [Wi-Fi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wi-Fi) connections can be found almost everywhere.[^cite_note-309] As of July 2022, 5G service is accessible in most of major cities. The UN ranks Greece among the top 30 countries with a highly developed information and communications infrastructure.[^cite_note-310]&#x20;

### Science and technology

Main article: [List of Greek inventions and discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Greek_inventions_and_discoveries)



[Georgios Papanikolaou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Papanikolaou), a pioneer in [cytopathology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytopathology) and early cancer detection

The General Secretariat for Research and Technology of the Ministry of Development and Competitiveness is responsible for designing, implementing and supervising national research and technological policy. In 2017, spending on research and development (R\&D) reached an all-time high of €2 billion, equal to 1.1% of GDP.[^cite_note-311]&#x20;

Greece was ranked 42nd in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]&#x20;

Greece has major technology parks with incubator facilities. The [Hellenic National Space Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_National_Space_Committee) began cooperating with the [European Space Agency](10363.md) (ESA) in 1994 and has been its member since 2005.[^cite_note-\:2-314] The country participates in the ESA's telecommunication and technology activities and the [Global Monitoring for Environment and Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Monitoring_for_Environment_and_Security) Initiative.[^cite_note-\:2-314] The [National Centre of Scientific Research "Demokritos"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Centre_of_Scientific_Research_%22Demokritos%22) was founded in 1959 and is the largest multidisciplinary research centre in Greece. Its activities cover several fields of science and engineering.[^cite_note-315]&#x20;

Greece has one of the highest rates of tertiary enrollment in the world,[^cite_note-316] while Greeks are well represented in academia worldwide; leading Western universities employ a disproportionately high number of Greek faculty.[^cite_note-317] Greek scientific publications have grown significantly in terms of [research impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Research_impact\&action=edit\&redlink=1), surpassing both the EU and global average from 2012 to 2016.[^cite_note-318]&#x20;

Notable Greek scientists of modern times include [Georgios Papanikolaou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Papanikolaou) (inventor of the [Pap test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pap_test)), mathematician [Constantin Carathéodory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantin_Carath%C3%A9odory) (known for the [Carathéodory theorems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carath%C3%A9odory_theorem_\(disambiguation\)) and [Carathéodory conjecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carath%C3%A9odory_conjecture)), astronomer [E. M. Antoniadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._M._Antoniadi), archaeologists [Ioannis Svoronos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Svoronos), [Valerios Stais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerios_Stais), [Spyridon Marinatos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Marinatos), [Manolis Andronikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manolis_Andronikos) (discovered the tomb of [Philip II of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon) in [Vergina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vergina)), Indologist [Dimitrios Galanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitrios_Galanos), botanist [Theodoros G. Orphanides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodoros_G._Orphanides), and scientists such as [Michael Dertouzos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dertouzos), [Nicholas Negroponte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Negroponte), [John Argyris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Argyris), [John Iliopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Iliopoulos) (2007 [Dirac Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirac_Medal_\(ICTP\)) for his contributions on the physics of the [charm quark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charm_quark)), [Joseph Sifakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Sifakis) (2007 [Turing Award](31093.md), the "Nobel Prize" of Computer Science), [Christos Papadimitriou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christos_Papadimitriou) (2002 [Knuth Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knuth_Prize), 2012 [Gödel Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6del_Prize)), [Mihalis Yannakakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihalis_Yannakakis) (2005 [Knuth Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knuth_Prize)) and physicist [Dimitri Nanopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitri_Nanopoulos).&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Greece) and [Greeks](42056.md)

[Eurostat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostat) estimated the Greek population at 10.6 million in 2022.[^cite_note-319]&#x20;



Greece population density, 2000

Greek society has changed significantly over recent decades, coinciding with the [broader European trend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aging_of_Europe) of declining fertility and population aging. The [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rate) of 1.4 children per woman is well below the [replacement rate of 2.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replacement_fertility_rate), and one of the lowest in the world, considerably below the high of 5.5 children in 1900.[^cite_note-320] The [crude birth rate](515898.md) in 2023 was 6.8 per 1,000 inhabitants, a significant decline from 8.5 per 1,000 in 2016 and 14.5 in 1981.[^cite_note-Factbook-Greece-161][^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322] The mortality rate in 2025 is 11.53 per 1,000 compared to 11.2 in 2016 and 8.9 in 1981.&#x20;

Greece's [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_age) is 44.2 years, the seventh highest in the world.[^cite_note-Factbook-Greece-161] In 2001, 17% of the population were 65 years old and older, 68% between the ages of 15 and 64 years old, and 15% were 14 years old and younger.[^cite_note-nssg-323] By 2023, those aged 65 and older had risen to 25.4%, while those aged 14 and younger declined to 6.4%; the population aged 15 to 64 rose slightly to 68.2%.[^cite_note-\:10-324] Marriage rates began declining from almost 71 per 1,000 inhabitants in 1981 to 51 in 2004.[^cite_note-nssg-323] Divorce rates have increased from 32.8 per 100 marriages in 2018 to 33.4 in 2022, albeit declining from the peak of 41.2 in 2020.[^cite_note-325]&#x20;

As a result of these trends, the average household is smaller and older than in previous generations. The economic crisis exacerbated this development, with 350,000–450,000 Greeks, predominantly young adults, emigrating since 2010,[^cite_note-326] when the population reached its peak of 11.1 million.[^cite_note-\:10-324] Based on current trends, the Greek population is projected to fall to 7.5 million by 2050.[^cite_note-327]&#x20;

### Cities

See also: [List of cities and towns in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Greece)

Almost two-thirds of the Greek people live in urban areas. Greece's largest and most influential metropolitan centres are [Athens](1216.md) (population 3,744,059 according to [2021 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athens_Metropolitan_Area,_Greece)) and [Thessaloniki](40471.md) (population 1,092,919 in [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area)), that latter commonly referred to as the *symprotévousa* (συμ[πρωτεύουσα](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CF%80%CF%81%CF%89%CF%84%CE%B5%CF%8D%CE%BF%CF%85%CF%83%CE%B1), lit. 'co-capital').[^cite_note-Greek_Experience-328] Other prominent cities with populations above 100,000 inhabitants include [Patras](470691.md), [Heraklion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heraklion), [Larissa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larissa), [Volos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volos), [Rhodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodes_\(city\)), [Ioannina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannina), [Agrinio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrinio), [Chania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chania), and [Chalcis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcis).[^cite_note-cities-329]&#x20;
