### Languages

Main articles: [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), [Varieties of Modern Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Modern_Greek), [Languages of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Greece), and [Minorities in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities_in_Greece)



Regions with a traditional presence of languages other than Greek. Today, Greek is the dominant language throughout the country.

[^cite_note-331]

[^cite_note-332]

[^cite_note-333]

[^cite_note-334]

[^cite_note-335]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrudgill2000-336]

Greece is relatively homogeneous in linguistic terms, with a large majority of the native population using Greek as their first or only language. Among the Greek-speaking population, speakers of the distinctive [Pontic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Greek) dialect came to Greece from Asia Minor after the [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide) and constitute a sizeable group. The [Cappadocian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappadocian_Greek) dialect came due to the genocide as well, but is endangered and barely spoken. Indigenous Greek dialects include the archaic Greek spoken by the [Sarakatsani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarakatsani), traditionally [transhumant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transhumance) mountain shepherds of [Greek Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Macedonia) and other parts of [Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Greece). The [Tsakonian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsakonian_language), a distinct Greek language derived from [Doric Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doric_Greek) instead of [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek), is still spoken in villages in the southeastern Peloponnese.&#x20;

The Muslim minority in Thrace, approximately 0.95% of the population, consists of speakers of [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), [Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_language) ([Pomaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomaks))[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrudgill2000-336] and [Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_language). Romani is spoken by Christian [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) in other parts of the country. The [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) has estimated that there are approximately 265,000 [Romani people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) living in Greece (2.47% of the population).[^cite_note-337] Other minority languages have traditionally been spoken by regional population groups in various areas. Their use decreased radically in the course of the 20th century through assimilation with the Greek-speaking majority. They are only maintained by the older generations and almost extinct. The same is true for the [Arvanites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arvanites), an [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language)-speaking group mostly located in rural areas around Athens, and for the [Aromanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aromanians) and [Megleno-Romanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megleno-Romanians) whose language is closely related to [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language) and who used to live scattered across areas of mountainous central Greece. Members of these groups usually identify ethnically as Greek[^cite_note-338] and are bilingual in Greek.&#x20;

Near the northern Greek borders there are some [Slavic–speaking groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_speakers_of_Greek_Macedonia), most of whom identify ethnically as Greeks. It is estimated that after the population exchanges of 1923, [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(Greece\)) had 200,000 to 400,000 [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_languages) speakers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoudometof2001186-339] The Jewish community traditionally spoke [Ladino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladino_language) (Judeo-Spanish), today maintained by a few thousand speakers. Other notable minority languages include [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language), [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language), and the Greco-Turkic dialect spoken by the [Urums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urums), a community of [Caucasus Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Greeks) from the [Tsalka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsalka) region of central Georgia and ethnic Greeks from southeastern [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Ukraine) who arrived in Northern Greece as economic migrants in the 1990s.&#x20;

### Migration

Main articles: [Greek diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora) and [Immigration to Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Greece)



A map of the fifty countries with the largest [Greek diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora) communities

Throughout the 20th century, millions of Greeks migrated to the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Americans), [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_the_United_Kingdom), [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Australians), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Canadians), and [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Germany), creating a large [Greek diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_diaspora). Net migration started to show positive numbers from the 1970s, but until the beginning of the 1990s, the main influx was returning Greek migrants or of [Pontic Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Greeks) and others from [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Russia), [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Georgia), [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Turkey) the [Czech Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_the_Czech_Republic), and elsewhere in the former [Soviet Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Bloc).[^cite_note-eliamep-340]&#x20;

A study from the Mediterranean Migration Observatory maintains that the 2001 census recorded 762,191 persons residing in Greece without Greek citizenship, constituting around 7% of the population. Of the non-citizen residents, 48,560 were EU or [European Free Trade Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association) nationals and 17,426 were Cypriots with privileged status. The majority came from Eastern European countries: Albania (56%), Bulgaria (5%), and Romania (3%), while migrants from the former Soviet Union (Georgia, Russia, Ukraine, Moldova, etc.) comprised 10% of the total.[^cite_note-341] The total Albanian national population, which included temporary migrants and undocumented persons, was around 600,000.[^cite_note-342]&#x20;

The [2011 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_census_2011) recorded 9,903,268 Greek citizens (92%), 480,824 Albanian citizens (4.4%), 75,915 Bulgarian citizens (0.7%), 46,523 Romanian citizenship (0.4%), 34,177 Pakistani citizens (0.3%), 27,400 [Georgian](48768.md) citizens (0.25%) and 247,090 people had other or unidentified citizenship (2%).[^cite_note-343] 189,000 people of the total population of Albanian citizens were reported in 2008 as ethnic Greeks from [Southern Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Albania), in the irredentist region of [Northern Epirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Epirus).[^cite_note-eliamep-340]&#x20;

The greatest cluster of non-EU immigrant population are in the larger urban centres, especially Athens, with 132,000 immigrants comprising 17% of the local population, and then Thessaloniki, with 27,000 immigrants reaching 7% of the local population. There is a considerable number of co-ethnics that came from the Greek communities of Albania and former [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-eliamep-340]&#x20;

Greece, together with Italy and Spain, is a major entry point for [illegal immigrants trying to enter the EU](46415102.md). Illegal immigrants entering mostly do so from the border with Turkey at the [Evros River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evros_River) and the islands of the eastern Aegean across from Turkey. In 2012, most illegal immigrants came from [Afghanistan](737.md), followed by Pakistanis and Bangladeshis.[^cite_note-344] In 2015, arrivals of refugees by sea had increased dramatically due to the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md). There were 856,723 arrivals by sea in Greece, an almost fivefold increase to the same period of 2014, of which the [Syrians](8862873.md) represented almost 45%.[^cite_note-345] Most refugees and migrants use Greece as a transit country to Northern Europe.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347] In July 2025, Greek officials implemented a three month suspension of asylum processing for migrants from North Africa, drawing criticism from human rights groups.[^cite_note-348]&#x20;

### Religion

Main articles: [Religion in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Greece), [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church), and [Church of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece)

See also: [Muslim minority of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_minority_of_Greece), [Hellenismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenismos), [Ancient Greek religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_religion), and [Romaniote Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews)

Religiosity in Greece (2017):

[^cite_note-Pew2017-1]

1. [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy) (90.0%)

2. Other Christians (exc.[Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholics)) (3.00%)

3. [Irreligion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) (4.00%)

4. [Islam](6037917.md) (2.00%)

5. Other religions (inc.[Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholics)) (1.00%)

The Greek Constitution recognises [Eastern Orthodoxy](10186.md) as the 'prevailing' faith of the country, while guaranteeing freedom of religious belief for all.[^cite_note-con51,53-184][^cite_note-Hri.org-349] The government does not keep statistics on religious groups and censuses do not ask for religious affiliation. According to the U.S. State Department, an estimated 97% of Greek citizens identify themselves as [Eastern Orthodox](10186.md), belonging to the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church),[^cite_note-religion-350] which uses the [Byzantine rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_rite) and the [Greek language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), the original language of the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament). The administration of the Greek territory is shared between the [Church of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece) and the [Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Constantinople).&#x20;

In a 2010 [Eurostat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostat)–[Eurobarometer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurobarometer) poll, 79% of Greek citizens responded that they "believe there is a God".[^cite_note-eurostat-351] According to other sources, 16% of Greeks describe themselves as "very religious", which is the highest among all European countries. The survey found just 3.5% never attend a church, compared to 5% in [Poland](22936.md) and 59% in the Czech Republic.[^cite_note-forskning.no-352] Estimates of the recognised [Muslim minority of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_minority_of_Greece), mostly located in [Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrace), range around 100,000,[^cite_note-religion-350][^cite_note-religion2-353] about 1% of the population. Some of the Albanian immigrants to Greece come from a nominally Muslim background, though most are secular.[^cite_note-354] Following the [1919–1922 Greco-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Turkish_War_\(1919%E2%80%931922\)) and the 1923 [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne), Greece and Turkey agreed to a [population transfer based on cultural and religious identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Greece_and_Turkey). About 500,000 Muslims from Greece, predominantly those defined as Turks, but also [Greek Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Muslims), were exchanged with approximately 1.5 million Greeks from Turkey. However, many refugees who settled in former Ottoman Muslim villages in [Central Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Macedonia), and were defined as Christian Orthodox [Caucasus Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Greeks), arrived from the former Russian [Transcaucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcaucasus) province of [Kars Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kars_Oblast), after it had been retroceded to Turkey prior to the population exchange.[^cite_note-355]&#x20;

Judaism has [been present](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Greece) in Greece for more than 2,000 years. The ancient community of Greek Jews is called [Romaniotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews), while the [Sephardi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) were once a prominent community in [Thessaloniki](40471.md), numbering some 80,000, or more than half of the population, by 1900.[^cite_note-356] However, after the [German occupation of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_occupation_of_Greece) and [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust), it is estimated to number around 5,500 people.[^cite_note-religion-350][^cite_note-religion2-353]&#x20;

The [Roman Catholic](606848.md) community is estimated to be around 250,000[^cite_note-religion-350][^cite_note-religion2-353] of which 50,000 are Greek citizens.[^cite_note-religion-350] [Their community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Greece) is nominally separate from the smaller [Greek Byzantine Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Byzantine_Catholic_Church), which recognises the primacy of the Pope but maintains the [liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy) of the [Byzantine Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Rite).[^cite_note-357] [Old Calendarists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Old_Calendarists) account for 500,000 followers.[^cite_note-religion2-353] Protestants, including the [Greek Evangelical Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Evangelical_Church) and [Free Evangelical Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Evangelical_Churches), stand at about 30,000.[^cite_note-religion-350][^cite_note-religion2-353] Other Christian minorities, such as [Assemblies of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblies_of_God), [International Church of the Foursquare Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Church_of_the_Foursquare_Gospel) and various [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism) churches of the [Greek Synod of Apostolic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Greek_Synod_of_Apostolic_Church\&action=edit\&redlink=1) total about 12,000 members.[^cite_note-358] The independent [Free Apostolic Church of Pentecost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Apostolic_Church_of_Pentecost) is the biggest Protestant denomination in Greece with 120 churches.[^cite_note-359] There are no official statistics about the Free Apostolic Church of Pentecost, but the Orthodox Church estimates the followers as 20,000.[^cite_note-360] The [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses) report having 28,874 active members.[^cite_note-361]&#x20;

Since 2017, [Hellenic Polytheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenism_\(religion\)), or Hellenism has been legally recognised as an actively practised religion,[^cite_note-362] with estimates of 2,000 active practitioners and an additional 100,000 "sympathisers".[^cite_note-newstatesman.com-363][^cite_note-telegraph.co.uk-364][^cite_note-365] Hellenism refers to religious movements that continue, revive, or reconstruct [ancient Greek religious practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_religion).&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Greece](420676.md)



The [Ionian Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionian_Academy) in [Corfu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corfu), the first [academic institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_institution) of modern Greece

Greeks have a long tradition of valuing and investing in *[paideia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paideia)* (education), which was upheld as one of the highest societal values in the Greek and Hellenistic world. The first European institution described as a university was founded in fifth-century Constantinople and continued operating in various incarnations until the city's fall to the Ottomans in 1453.[^cite_note-texor-366] The [University of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Constantinople) was Christian Europe's first secular institution of higher learning,[^cite_note-367] and by some measures was the world's first university.[^cite_note-texor-366]&#x20;

Compulsory education in Greece comprises primary schools (Δημοτικό Σχολείο, *Dimotikó Scholeio*) and [gymnasium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnasium_\(school\)) (Γυμνάσιο). Nursery schools (Παιδικός σταθμός, *Paidikós Stathmós*) are popular but not compulsory. [Kindergartens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kindergarten) (Νηπιαγωγείο, *Nipiagogeío*) are compulsory for any child above four. Children start primary school aged six and remain there for six years. Attendance at gymnasia starts aged 12 and lasts for three years.&#x20;

Greece's post-compulsory secondary education consists of two main school types: unified upper secondary schools (Γενικό Λύκειο, *Genikό Lykeiό*) and [technical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technical_school)–[vocational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_school) schools. The traditional Technical-Vocational Educational Schools (Τεχνικά και Επαγγελματικά Εκπαιδευτήρια, "TEE") have been replaced by two pathways: Vocational Lyceums (Επαγγελματικό Λύκειο, "EPAL") and Vocational Training Schools (Επαγγελματικές Σχολές, "EPAS"). Public higher education consists of Highest Educational Institutions (Ανώτατα Εκπαιδευτικά Ιδρύματα, "ΑΕΙ") and Schools of Advanced Vocational Training (Σχολές Ανώτερης Επαγγελματικής Κατάρτισης, "SAEK"). The Technological Education Institutions (TEI) have been incorporated into existing universities or merged into new universities.[^cite_note-368]&#x20;



The new National Library of Greece at the [Stavros Niarchos Foundation Cultural Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stavros_Niarchos_Foundation_Cultural_Center)

The higher education landscape has seen a major shift with the enactment of Law 5094/2024, which for the first time establishes a framework for the licensing and operation of non-profit University-Legal Entities (ULEs) in Greece, affiliated with recognized foreign universities, starting from the 2025–2026 academic year.[^cite_note-369]&#x20;

Students are admitted to public AEI Institutes according to their performance at national level examinations taking place after completion of the third grade of Lykeio. Students over 22 may be admitted to the Hellenic Open University through a lottery.&#x20;

The education system provides special kindergartens, primary, and secondary schools for people with special needs or difficulties in learning. There are specialist gymnasia and high schools offering musical, theological, and physical education.&#x20;

72% of adults aged 25–64 have completed upper secondary education, which is slightly less than the OECD average of 74%. The average Greek pupil scored 458 in reading literacy, maths and science in the OECD's 2015 Programme for International Student Assessment (PISA). This is lower than the OECD average of 486. Girls outperformed boys by 15 points, much more than the average OECD gap of 2.[^cite_note-370]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Greece)

See also: [Healthcare in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Greece)

Greece has [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care). The system is mixed, combining a national health service with [social health insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_health_insurance) (SHI). Per a 2000 [World Health Organisation](33583.md) report, its [health system](60976.md) ranked 14th in overall performance of 191 countries surveyed.[^cite_note-WHO_report-371] In a 2013 [Save the Children](436885.md) report, Greece was ranked the 19th out of 176 countries for the state of mothers and newborn babies.[^cite_note-Save_the_Children_report-372] As of 2014, there were 124 public hospitals, of which 106 were general hospitals and 18 specialised hospitals, with a total capacity of about 30,000 beds.[^cite_note-373]&#x20;

Greece's health care expenditures was 9.6% of GDP in 2007. By 2015, it declined to 8.4%, compared with the EU average of 9.5%. Nevertheless, the country maintains the highest doctor-to-population ratio of any OECD country[^cite_note-OECD-374] and the highest doctor-to-patient ratio in the EU.[^cite_note-\:0-375]&#x20;

[Life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) is among the highest in the world; life expectancy in 2015 was 81.1 years, slightly above the EU average of 80.6.[^cite_note-\:0-375] The island of [Icaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icaria) has the highest percentage of nonagenarians in the world; 33% of islanders are 90 or older.[^cite_note-NPR-376] Icaria is subsequently classified as a "[Blue Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Zone)", a region where people allegedly live longer than average and have lower rates of cancer, heart disease, or other chronic illnesses.[^cite_note-NYT-377]&#x20;

A 2011, OECD report showed Greece had the largest percentage of adult daily smokers of any of the 34 OECD members.[^cite_note-OECD-374] The obesity rate is 18%, above the OECD average of 15%.[^cite_note-OECD-374]&#x20;

In 2008, infant mortality, with a rate of 3.6 deaths per 1,000 live births, was below the 2007 OECD average of 4.9.[^cite_note-OECD-374]&#x20;

## Culture

Main articles: [Culture of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Greece), [Greeks](42056.md), and [List of Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Greeks)



The [Ancient Theatre of Epidaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Theatre_of_Epidaurus), [annually used for theatrical plays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidaurus_Festival) since 1955

The culture of Greece has evolved, beginning in [Mycenaean Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece) and continuing into [Classical Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece), through the influence of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) and its [Greek Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_East) continuation, the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire). Other cultures and nations, such as the [Latin and Frankish states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankokratia), the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), the [Venetian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_Republic), the [Genoese Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genoese_Republic), and the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) have left their influence on modern Greek culture, though historians credit the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence) with revitalising Greece and giving birth to a single, cohesive entity of its multifaceted culture.&#x20;

In ancient times, Greece was the birthplace of [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture).[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-Broad2007-379] Modern democracies owe a debt to Greek beliefs in government by the people, trial by jury, and equality under the law. The ancient Greeks pioneered in many fields that rely on systematic thought, including [logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic#History), [biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology), [geometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geometry), government, [geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography), medicine, history,[^cite_note-380] philosophy,[^cite_note-381] [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics), and mathematics.[^cite_note-Heath1981-382] They introduced important literary forms as epic and lyrical poetry, history, tragedy, comedy and drama. In their pursuit of order and proportion, the Greeks created an ideal of beauty that strongly influenced [Western art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_art_history).[^cite_note-383]&#x20;

### Visual arts

See also: [Greek art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_art), [Byzantine art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_art), and [Modern Greek art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_art)



Close-up of the *Charioteer of [Delphi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delphi)*, a celebrated statue from the 5th century BC

Artistic production in Greece began in the prehistoric pre-Greek [Cycladic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycladic_civilization) and the [Minoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoan_civilization) civilisations, both of which were influenced by local traditions and the [art of ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_ancient_Egypt).[^cite_note-384]&#x20;

There were interconnected traditions of painting in ancient Greece. Due to technical differences, they underwent differentiated developments. Not all painting techniques are equally well represented in the [archaeological](18951655.md) record. The most respected form of art, according to [Pliny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder) or [Pausanias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pausanias_\(geographer\)), were individual, mobile paintings on wooden boards, described as [panel paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panel_painting). Wall painting in Greece goes back at least to the [Minoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoan_civilization) and [Mycenaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece) civilisations, with the lavish fresco decoration of sites like [Knossos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knossos), [Tiryns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiryns), and [Mycenae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenae).&#x20;



An example of Macedonian-era ivorywork from Constantinople: the [Forty Martyrs of Sebaste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forty_Martyrs_of_Sebaste), now in the [Bode Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bode_Museum), [Berlin](3354.md)

[Ancient Greek sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_sculpture) was composed almost entirely of workable and durable materials, [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble) or [bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze), bronze becoming the favoured medium for major works by the early 5th century, while [chryselephantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chryselephantine) sculptures, made largely of [gold](12240.md) and [ivory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivory) and used for temple [cult images](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_image) and luxury works, were much rarer. It has been established that ancient Greek sculptures were painted[^cite_note-Gurewitsch-385] with a variety of colours, a feature known as [polychromy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polychromy).[^cite_note-386]&#x20;

Art production continued during the Byzantine era. The most salient feature of this new aesthetic was its "abstract", or anti-naturalistic character. While classical art was marked by attempts to create representations that mimicked reality, Byzantine art favoured a more symbolic approach. Byzantine painting concentrated mainly on [icons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) and [hagiographies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagiography). The [Macedonian art (Byzantine)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_art_\(Byzantine\)) was the artistic expression of the [Macedonian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_Renaissance), a label used to describe the Macedonian dynasty of the Byzantine Empire (867–1056), which scholars have seen as a time of increased interest in classical scholarship and the assimilation of classical motifs into [Christian artwork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_art).[^cite_note-387]&#x20;

Post Byzantine art schools include the [Cretan School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretan_School) and [Heptanese School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heptanese_School_\(painting\)). The first artistic movement in the [Greek Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Kingdom) can be considered the [Greek academic art of the 19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_academic_art_of_the_19th_century) (*Munich School*). Modern Greek painters include [Nikolaos Gyzis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolaos_Gyzis), [Georgios Jakobides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Jakobides), [Theodoros Vryzakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodoros_Vryzakis), [Nikiforos Lytras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikiforos_Lytras), [Konstantinos Volanakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantinos_Volanakis), [Nikos Engonopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Engonopoulos) and [Yannis Tsarouchis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yannis_Tsarouchis), while notable sculptors are [Pavlos Prosalentis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlos_Prosalentis), [Ioannis Kossos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Kossos), [Leonidas Drosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonidas_Drosis), [Georgios Bonanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Bonanos), and [Yannoulis Chalepas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yannoulis_Chalepas).&#x20;

### Architecture

See also: [Ancient Greek architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_architecture), [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture), and [Modern Greek architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_architecture)



Harbour of [Ermoupolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermoupolis), on the island [Syros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syros), first capital of independent Greece.



Narrow path between village houses on the island of [Astypalea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astypalea).

The architecture of ancient Greece was produced by the ancient Greeks (*Hellenes*), whose [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) flourished on the Greek mainland, the [Aegean Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Islands) and their [colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonies_in_antiquity), from about 900 BC until the 1st century AD, with the earliest remaining architectural works dating from around 600 BC. The formal vocabulary of ancient Greek architecture, in particular the division of architectural style into three defined orders (the [Doric Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doric_Order), the [Ionic Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_Order), and the [Corinthian Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corinthian_Order)), was to have a profound effect on [Western architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_architecture).&#x20;

Byzantine architecture was dominant in the Greek-speaking world and significantly influenced [Medieval architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_architecture) throughout Europe and the Near East, becoming the primary progenitor of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_architecture) and [Ottoman architectural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture) traditions that followed the Byzantine Empire's collapse.&#x20;

After Greek Independence, modern Greek architects combined traditional Greek and Byzantine elements and motives with Western European movements and styles. [Patras](470691.md) was the first city of the modern Greek state to develop a city plan applying the orthogonal rule by [Stamatis Voulgaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamatis_Voulgaris), a Greek engineer of the French army, in 1829.[^cite_note-388]&#x20;

Two special genres can be considered the Cycladic architecture, featuring white-coloured houses, in the [Cyclades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclades) and the Epirotic architecture in the region of [Epirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epirus).[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] Important is also the influence of the [Venetian style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_Gothic_architecture) in the [Ionian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionian_Islands) and the "Mediterranean style" of [Florestano Di Fausto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florestano_Di_Fausto) (during the fascist regime) in the [Dodecanese islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodecanese).[^cite_note-391]&#x20;

After the establishment of the [Greek Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Kingdom), the architecture of Athens and other cities was mostly influenced by the [Neoclassical architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture). For Athens, the first [King of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Greece), [Otto of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_of_Greece), commissioned the architects [Stamatios Kleanthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamatios_Kleanthis) and [Eduard Schaubert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Schaubert) to design a modern city plan fit for a capital. After the [Great Thessaloniki Fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917), the government ordered for a new city plan under the supervision of [Ernest Hébrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_H%C3%A9brard). Other modern Greek architects include [Anastasios Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anastasios_Metaxas), [Lysandros Kaftanzoglou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysandros_Kaftanzoglou), [Panagis Kalkos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panagis_Kalkos), [Ernst Ziller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Ziller), [Xenophon Paionidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophon_Paionidis), [Dimitris Pikionis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitris_Pikionis), and [Georges Candilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Candilis).&#x20;

There is an emerging need to secure the long-term preservation of the archaeological sites and monuments against the growing threats of climate change.[^cite_note-392]&#x20;

### Theatre

See also: [Theatre of ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_ancient_Greece) and [Modern Greek theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_theatre)



[Nobile Teatro di San Giacomo di Corfù](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobile_Teatro_di_San_Giacomo_di_Corf%C3%B9), the first theatre and opera house of modern Greece

Theatre in its Western form was born in Greece.[^cite_note-393] [Tragedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_tragedy) (late 6th century BC), [comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_comedy) (486 BC), and the [satyr play](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satyr_play) were the three dramatic genres that emerged in the [city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis) of [Classical Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Athens) and were institutionalised as part of a festival called the [Dionysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysia), which honoured the god [Dionysus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysus). Of the hundreds of [tragedies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tragedies) written and performed during the classical age, only a limited number of plays by three authors have survived: [Aeschylus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeschylus), [Sophocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophocles), and [Euripides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euripides). The surviving plays by [Aristophanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristophanes) are a treasure trove of comic presentation.&#x20;

During the Byzantine period, theatrical art declined; the only form that survived was folk theatre (*Mimos* and *Pantomimos*), despite the hostility of the state.[^cite_note-394] During the Ottoman period, the main theatrical folk art was the *[Karagiozis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karagiozis)*. The Renaissance, which led to the modern Greek theatre, took place in the [Venetian Crete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_Crete). Significant dramatists of the era include [Vitsentzos Kornaros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitsentzos_Kornaros) and [Georgios Chortatzis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Chortatzis).&#x20;

Modern Greek theatre was born after independence, in the early 19th century, and initially was influenced by Heptanesean theatre and melodrama, such as the Italian opera. The [Nobile Teatro di San Giacomo di Corfù](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobile_Teatro_di_San_Giacomo_di_Corf%C3%B9) was the first theatre and opera house of modern Greece and the place where the first Greek opera, [Spyridon Xyndas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Xyndas)' *The Parliamentary Candidate* was performed. During the late 19th and early 20th century, the Athenian theatre scene was dominated by [revues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revues), [musical comedies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_theatre), [operettas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operettas) and [nocturnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturnes) and notable playwrights included [Spyridon Samaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Samaras), [Dionysios Lavrangas](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dionysios_Lavrangas\&action=edit\&redlink=1), [Theophrastos Sakellaridis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastos_Sakellaridis).&#x20;

The [National Theatre of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_of_Greece) was opened in 1900 as *Royal Theatre*.[^cite_note-395] Notable playwrights of the modern Greek theatre include [Gregorios Xenopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorios_Xenopoulos), [Nikos Kazantzakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Kazantzakis), [Pantelis Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantelis_Horn), [Alekos Sakellarios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alekos_Sakellarios), and [Iakovos Kambanellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iakovos_Kambanellis), while notable actors include [Cybele Andrianou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cybele_Andrianou), [Marika Kotopouli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marika_Kotopouli), [Aimilios Veakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aimilios_Veakis), [Orestis Makris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orestis_Makris), [Katina Paxinou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katina_Paxinou), [Manos Katrakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manos_Katrakis), and [Dimitris Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitris_Horn). Significant directors include [Dimitris Rontiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitris_Rontiris), [Alexis Minotis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_Minotis), and [Karolos Koun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karolos_Koun).&#x20;

### Literature

Main articles: [Greek literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_literature), [Byzantine literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_literature), and [Modern Greek literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_literature)







[Constantine P. Cavafy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_P._Cavafy)'s work was inspired mainly by the [Hellenistic past](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period), while [Odysseas Elytis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odysseas_Elytis) (*centre*) and [Giorgos Seferis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgos_Seferis) (*right*) were representatives of the Generation of the '30s and [Nobel laureates in Literature](23385442.md)

Greek literature can be divided into three main categories: Ancient, Byzantine, and modern Greek.[^cite_note-britannica-396] Athens is considered the birthplace of Western literature.[^cite_note-Strickland2007-397] At the beginning of Greek literature stand the monumental works of [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer): the *[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad)* and the *[Odyssey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odyssey)*, composed around 800 BC or after. In the classical period, many of the genres of Western literature became more prominent. [Lyrical poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyrical_poetry), [odes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ode), [pastorals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastorals), [elegies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elegy), [epigrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigrams); dramatic presentations of comedy and [tragedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tragedy); [historiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography), [rhetorical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetorical) treatises, philosophical dialectics, and philosophical treatises all arose in this period. The two major lyrical poets were [Sappho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sappho) and [Pindar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pindar). [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) and [Thucydides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thucydides) are two of the most influential historians in this period.&#x20;

[Byzantine literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_literature) written in [Attic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attic_Greek), [Medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval) and early [Modern Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek), is the expression of the intellectual life of the [Byzantine Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Greeks) during the Christian [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages). Although *popular* Byzantine literature and early [Modern Greek literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_literature) both began in the 11th century, the two are indistinguishable.[^cite_note-398]&#x20;

[Modern Greek literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_literature) refers to literature written in the common Modern Greek language, emerging in the late Byzantine period of the 11th century. The Cretan Renaissance poem *[Erotokritos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erotokritos)* is considered the masterpiece of this period. It is a verse [romance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_\(heroic_literature\)) written around 1600 by [Vitsentzos Kornaros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitsentzos_Kornaros) (1553–1613). Later, during the period of Greek enlightenment ([Diafotismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diafotismos)), writers such as [Adamantios Korais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamantios_Korais) and [Rigas Feraios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigas_Feraios) prepared with their works the Greek Revolution.&#x20;

Leading figures of modern Greek literature include [Dionysios Solomos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysios_Solomos), [Andreas Kalvos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Kalvos), [Angelos Sikelianos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelos_Sikelianos), [Emmanuel Rhoides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Rhoides), [Demetrius Vikelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demetrius_Vikelas), [Kostis Palamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kostis_Palamas), [Penelope Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penelope_Delta), [Yannis Ritsos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yannis_Ritsos), [Alexandros Papadiamantis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandros_Papadiamantis), [Nikos Kazantzakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Kazantzakis), [Andreas Embirikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Embirikos), [Kostas Karyotakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kostas_Karyotakis), [Gregorios Xenopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorios_Xenopoulos), [Constantine Cavafy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_P._Cavafy), [Nikos Kavvadias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Kavvadias), [Kostas Varnalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kostas_Varnalis), and [Kiki Dimoula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiki_Dimoula). Two Greek authors have been awarded the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md): [George Seferis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Seferis) in 1963, and [Odysseas Elytis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odysseas_Elytis) in 1979.&#x20;

### Philosophy

Main articles: [Ancient Greek philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_philosophy) and [Modern Greek Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_Enlightenment)



Statues of [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato) (left) and [Socrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socrates) (right) in front of the [Academy of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_Athens_\(modern\)), Greece's [national academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_academy) and highest research establishment.

[Ancient Greek philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_philosophy) involved a disposition to value [reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reasoning) and [thinking critical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_thinking)ly of traditional culture, thus inaugurating the [Western intellectual tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture). While [thinkers before him](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presocratics) provided proto-scientific explanations of the natural world, [Socrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socrates) in 5th-century Athens systematically enquired [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics); the next century, his disciple, [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato), wrote presently still pertinent dialogues about [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics), [politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_philosophy), [metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics), and [epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemology). There were also topics of treatises composed by Plato's prolific student, [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle), whose thought, especially in [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_physics), [infused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelianism) the [West](21208200.md) for centuries. Other philosophical schools emerged during the Hellenistic period, [Cynicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynicism_\(philosophy\)), [Stoicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoicism), [Epicureanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epicureanism) and [Skepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skepticism), while [Neoplatonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonism) dominated subsequent thought.[^cite_note-399]&#x20;

[Byzantine philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_philosophy) was characterised by a Christian world-view, but one which could draw ideas directly from the Greek texts of Plato, Aristotle, and the [Neoplatonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonists). On the eve of the [Fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople), [Gemistus Pletho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gemistus_Pletho) tried to restore the use of the term "Hellene" and advocated the return to the [Olympian Gods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympian_Gods) of the ancient world.[^cite_note-400] Byzantine Greek scholars, who were largely responsible for preserving [Classical Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece) knowledge, fled to the West after the [fall of Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople), taking with them literature and significantly [contributing to the Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_scholars_in_the_Renaissance).[^cite_note-JJN-401]&#x20;

In the modern period, [Diafotismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Greek_Enlightenment) (Greek: Διαφωτισμός, "enlightenment", "illumination")[^cite_note-402] was the Greek expression of the [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) and its philosophical and political ideas. Notable representatives were [Adamantios Korais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamantios_Korais), [Rigas Feraios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigas_Feraios) and [Theophilos Kairis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophilos_Kairis). Other modern era Greek philosophers or political scientists include [Helle Lambridis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elli_Lambridi), [Cornelius Castoriadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornelius_Castoriadis), [Nicos Poulantzas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicos_Poulantzas) and [Christos Yannaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christos_Yannaras).&#x20;

### Music and dances

Main article: [Music of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Greece)



[Cretan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretan) dancers of [traditional folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_folk_music)

Greek vocal music dates back to ancient times, when mixed-gender choruses performed for entertainment, celebration, and spiritual reasons. Instruments included the double-reed [aulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aulos) and the plucked string instrument, the [lyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyre), especially the special kind called a [kithara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kithara). While the new technique of polyphony was developing in the West, the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md) resisted change. Therefore, [Byzantine music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_music) remained monophonic and without any form of instrumental accompaniment. As a result, and despite certain attempts by certain Greek chanters, Byzantine music was deprived of elements which, in the West, encouraged an unimpeded development of art. Byzantium presented the monophonic [Byzantine chant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_chant), a melodic music, with rhythmical variety and expressive power.[^cite_note-403]&#x20;

Along with Byzantine chant and music, the Greeks cultivated the [Greek folk song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_folk_music) (*Demotiko*), which is divided into two cycles, the [akritic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acritic_songs) and [klephtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klephtic_song). The akritic was created between the 9th and 10th centuries and depicted the life and struggles of the [akrites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akrites) (frontier guards) of the Byzantine Empire, the most well-known of whom was [Digenes Akritas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digenes_Akritas). The klephtic cycle came into being between the late Byzantine period and the start of the Greek War of Independence. The most famous klephtic and modern Greek folk song is *The Battle of Mount Olympus and Mount Kisavos*, a ballad based on a musico-poetic motif dating back to classical Greece.[^cite_note-Trypanis-404] The klephtic cycle, together with historical songs, *paraloghes* (narrative song or ballad), love songs, [mantinades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantinada), wedding songs, songs of exile, and dirges express the life of the Greeks.&#x20;



[Mikis Theodorakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikis_Theodorakis) was one of the most popular and significant Greek composers.

The [Heptanesean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heptanese) [kantádhes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_folk_music#Ionian_Islands) (καντάδες '[serenades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serenade)'; sing.: καντάδα) became the forerunners of the Greek modern urban popular song, influencing its development. For the first part of the next century, Greek composers continued to borrow elements from the Heptanesean style. The most successful songs during 1870–1930 were the so-called Athenian serenades, and the songs performed on stage ('theatrical revue songs') in [revues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revue), [operettas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operetta), and [nocturnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturne) that dominated Athens' theatre scene.[^cite_note-\:9-405]&#x20;

[Rebetiko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebetiko), initially a music associated with the lower classes, later gained greater acceptance as the rough edges of its overt subcultural character were softened, sometimes to the point of unrecognisability.[^cite_note-406] It was the base of the later [laïkó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%C3%AFk%C3%B3) (song of the people). The leading performers of the genre include [Vassilis Tsitsanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassilis_Tsitsanis), [Grigoris Bithikotsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigoris_Bithikotsis), [Stelios Kazantzidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stelios_Kazantzidis), [George Dalaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Dalaras), [Haris Alexiou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haris_Alexiou) and [Glykeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glykeria).&#x20;

It was through the [Ionian islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionian_islands) (which were under western rule) that major advances of the western European classical music were introduced to mainland Greeks. The region is notable for the birth of the first school of modern Greek classical music ([Heptanesean or Ionian School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionian_School_\(music\))), established in 1815. Prominent representatives of this genre include [Nikolaos Mantzaros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolaos_Mantzaros), [Spyridon Xyndas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Xyndas), [Spyridon Samaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Samaras) and [Pavlos Carrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlos_Carrer). [Manolis Kalomiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manolis_Kalomiris) is considered the founder of the Greek National School of Music.[^cite_note-\:9-405]&#x20;

In the 20th century, Greek composers had a significant impact on the development of [avant garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant_garde) and modern classical music, with figures such as [Iannis Xenakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iannis_Xenakis), [Nikos Skalkottas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Skalkottas), and [Dimitri Mitropoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitri_Mitropoulos) achieving international prominence. Composers and musicians such as [Mikis Theodorakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikis_Theodorakis), [Manos Hatzidakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manos_Hatzidakis), [Eleni Karaindrou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleni_Karaindrou), [Vangelis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vangelis) and [Demis Roussos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demis_Roussos) garnered an international following, which include famous [film scores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_score) such as *[Zorba the Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zorba_the_Greek)*, *[Serpico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serpico)*, *[Never on Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Never_on_Sunday)*, *[America America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_America)*, *[Eternity and a Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternity_and_a_Day)*, *[Chariots of Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chariots_of_Fire)*, and *[Blade Runner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blade_Runner_\(soundtrack\))*. [Greek American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_American) composers known for their film scores include [Yanni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanni) and [Basil Poledouris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil_Poledouris). Greek opera singers and classical musicians of the 20th and 21st century include [Maria Callas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Callas), [Nana Mouskouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nana_Mouskouri), [Mario Frangoulis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Frangoulis), [Leonidas Kavakos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonidas_Kavakos), and [Dimitris Sgouros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitris_Sgouros).[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409]&#x20;

During the [Greek junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_junta) of 1967–74, the music of Mikis Theodorakis was banned, the composer jailed, internally exiled, and put in a [concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration_camp),[^cite_note-Hischak2015-410] before finally being allowed to leave Greece due to international reaction. Released during the junta years, *Make Love, Stop the Gunfire*, by pop group [Poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll_\(band\)) is considered the first anti-war protest song in [Greek rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_rock).[^cite_note-europopmusic.eu-411]&#x20;

Greece participated in the [Eurovision Song Contest](9954.md) 35 times after its debut at the [1974 Contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_1974). In [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2005), Greece won with "[My Number One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Number_One)", performed by Greek-Swedish singer [Elena Paparizou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Paparizou), which became a smash hit in different countries and especially in Greece, and the [51st Eurovision Song Contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2006) of 2006 was held in Athens.[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413]&#x20;

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Greece)



[Theodoros Angelopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodoros_Angelopoulos), winner of the [Palme d'Or](612946.md) in [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Cannes_Film_Festival), notable director in the history of the [European cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_cinema)

Cinema first appeared in Greece in 1896, but the first cine-theatre was opened in 1907 in Athens. In 1914, the *Asty Films Company* was founded and the production of long films began. *Golfo*, a well known traditional love story, is considered the first Greek feature film, although there were minor productions such as newscasts before. In 1931, [Orestis Laskos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orestis_Laskos) directed *[Daphnis and Chloe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daphnis_and_Chloe_\(film\))*, containing one of the first nude scene in European cinema;[^cite_note-414] it was the first Greek movie played abroad.[^cite_note-415] In 1944, [Katina Paxinou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katina_Paxinou) was honoured with the [Best Supporting Actress Academy Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Supporting_Actress) for *[For Whom the Bell Tolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_Whom_the_Bell_Tolls_\(film\))*.[^cite_note-416]&#x20;

The 1950s and early 1960s are considered to be a "golden age" of Greek cinema.[^cite_note-417] Directors and actors of this era were recognised as important figures in Greece and some gained international acclaim: [George Tzavellas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Tzavellas), [Irene Papas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irene_Papas), [Melina Mercouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melina_Mercouri), [Michael Cacoyannis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cacoyannis), [Alekos Sakellarios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alekos_Sakellarios), [Nikos Tsiforos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Tsiforos), [Iakovos Kambanelis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iakovos_Kambanelis), [Katina Paxinou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katina_Paxinou), [Nikos Koundouros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Koundouros), [Ellie Lambeti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellie_Lambeti) and others. More than sixty films per year were made, with most having film noir elements. Notable films include *[The Drunkard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Drunkard_\(1950_film\))* (1950, directed by [George Tzavellas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Tzavellas)), *[The Counterfeit Coin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Counterfeit_Coin)* (1955, by [Giorgos Tzavellas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgos_Tzavellas)), *Πικρό Ψωμί* (1951, by [Grigoris Grigoriou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigoris_Grigoriou)), *[O Drakos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Drakos)* (1956, by [Nikos Koundouros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Koundouros)), *[Stella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stella_\(1955_film\))* (1955, directed by Cacoyannis and written by Kampanellis), *[Woe to the Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woe_to_the_Young)* (1961, by [Alekos Sakellarios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alekos_Sakellarios)), *[Glory Sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glory_Sky)* (1962, by [Takis Kanellopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takis_Kanellopoulos)) and *[The Red Lanterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Red_Lanterns)* (1963, by [Vasilis Georgiadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasilis_Georgiadis))&#x20;

Cacoyannis directed *[Zorba the Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zorba_the_Greek_\(film\))* with Anthony Quinn which received Best Director, Best Adapted Screenplay, and Best Film nominations.[^cite_note-418] [Finos Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finos_Film) contributed in this period with movies such as *Λατέρνα, Φτώχεια και Φιλότιμο*, *[Madalena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madalena_\(1960_film\))*, *[I theia ap' to Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_theia_ap%27_to_Chicago)*, *Το ξύλο βγήκε από τον Παράδεισο* and many more.&#x20;

During the 1970s and 1980s, [Theo Angelopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theo_Angelopoulos) directed notable movies. His film *[Eternity and a Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternity_and_a_Day)* won the [Palme d'Or](612946.md) and the [Prize of the Ecumenical Jury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prize_of_the_Ecumenical_Jury) at the [1998 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Cannes_Film_Festival).[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421]&#x20;

There are internationally renowned filmmakers in the Greek diaspora, such as the Greek-French [Costa-Gavras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa-Gavras) and the Greek-Americans [Elia Kazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elia_Kazan), [John Cassavetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Cassavetes), and [Alexander Payne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Payne). [Yorgos Lanthimos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorgos_Lanthimos) has received four [Academy Award](324.md) nominations for his work, including [Best Foreign Language Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Foreign_Language_Film) for *[Dogtooth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogtooth_\(film\))* (2009), [Best Original Screenplay](47125790.md) for *[The Lobster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lobster)* (2015), and [Best Picture](61702.md) and [Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director) for *[The Favourite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Favourite_\(2018_film\))* (2018).[^cite_note-422]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main articles: [Greek cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_cuisine) and [Greek wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_wine)



A [Greek salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_salad), with [feta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feta) and [olives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive)

[Greek cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_cuisine) is characteristic of the [Mediterranean diet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_diet), which is epitomised by dishes of [Crete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretan_diet).[^cite_note-423] Greek cuisine incorporates fresh ingredients into local dishes such as [moussaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moussaka), [pastitsio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastitsio), classic [Greek salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_salad), [fasolada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasolada), [spanakopita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanakopita) and [souvlaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Souvlaki). Some dishes can be traced back to ancient Greece like [skordalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skordalia) (a thick purée of walnuts, almonds, crushed garlic and olive oil), [lentil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lentil) [soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soup), [retsina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retsina) (white or rosé wine sealed with pine resin) and [pasteli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame_seed_candy) (candy bar with sesame seeds baked with honey). People often enjoy eating from small dishes such as [meze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meze) with dips such as [tzatziki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzatziki), grilled octopus and small fish, [feta cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feta_cheese), [dolmades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmades) (rice, currants and pine kernels wrapped in vine leaves), various [pulses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legume), [olives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive) and cheese. [Olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) is a widespread addition.[^cite_note-\:4-424]&#x20;

Sweet desserts include [melomakarona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melomakarona), [diples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diples) and [galaktoboureko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaktoboureko), and drinks such as [ouzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ouzo), [metaxa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaxa) and wines including retsina. Greek cuisine differs from different parts of the mainland and island to island. It uses some flavourings more often than other Mediterranean cuisines: [oregano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregano), [mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mentha), garlic, onion, [dill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dill) and [bay laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_laurel) leaves. Other common herbs and spices include [basil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil), [thyme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thyme) and [fennel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fennel) seed. Many recipes, especially in the northern parts of the country, use "sweet" spices in combination with meat, for example [cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon) and [cloves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clove) in stews.[^cite_note-425][^cite_note-\:4-424] [Koutoukia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koutoukia) are an underground restaurant common in Greece.[^cite_note-\:02-426]&#x20;

### Sports



[Spyridon Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spyridon_Louis) entering the [Panathenaic Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panathenaic_Stadium) at the end of the marathon; [1896 Summer Olympics](48644.md)



[Angelos Charisteas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelos_Charisteas) scoring Greece's winning goal in the [UEFA Euro 2004 Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2004_Final)

Main article: [Sports in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Greece)

Greece is the birthplace of the [ancient Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Olympic_Games), first recorded in 776 BC in [Olympia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia,_Greece), and hosted the modern [Olympic Games](22576.md) twice, the inaugural [1896 Summer Olympics](48644.md) and the [2004 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics). During the parade of nations, Greece is always called first, as the founding nation of the ancient precursor of the modern Olympics. The nation has competed at every [Summer Olympic Games](27566.md), one of only four countries to have done so. Having won a total of 129 medals (36 gold, 46 silver and 47 bronze), Greece is ranked 35th by gold medals in the [all-time Summer Olympic medal count](3729318.md). Their best ever performance was in the 1896 Summer Olympics, when Greece finished second in the [medal table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1896_Summer_Olympics_medal_table) with 10 gold medals.&#x20;

The [Greece national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greece_national_football_team), ranked 47th in the [world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_World_Rankings) as of 2024 (and having reached a high of 8th in 2008 and 2011),[^cite_note-427] were crowned [European Champions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_European_Championship#Teams_reaching_the_final) in [Euro 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2004) in one of the biggest upsets in the history of the sport.[^cite_note-Euro2004-428] The [Greek Super League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_League_Greece) is the highest professional football league, comprising fourteen teams. The most successful are [Olympiacos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiacos_F.C.), [Panathinaikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panathinaikos_F.C.), and [AEK Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AEK_Athens_F.C.).&#x20;

The [Greek national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greece_national_basketball_team) has a decades-long tradition of excellence and is considered among the world's top basketball powers. As of 2026, it ranked 12th in the [world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_World_Rankings) and 7th in [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Europe).[^cite_note-429] They have won the [European Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket) twice in [1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_1987) and [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_2005),[^cite_note-430] and have reached the final four in two of the last four [FIBA World Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_World_Championship), taking the second place in the world in [2006 FIBA World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_FIBA_World_Championship). The domestic top basketball league, [A1 Ethniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_Ethniki), has fourteen teams. The most successful Greek teams are [Panathinaikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panathinaikos_BC), [Olympiacos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiacos_B.C.), [Aris Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aris_B.C.), [AEK Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AEK_Athens_B.C.) and [PAOK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P.A.O.K._B.C.). Greek basketball teams are the [most successful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroleague#Finals) in [European basketball the last 25 years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroleague#Titles). After the [2005 European Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_EuroBasket_2005) triumph of the Greek national basketball team, Greece became the reigning European Champion in both football and basketball.&#x20;

The [Greece women's national water polo team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greece_women%27s_national_water_polo_team) have emerged as one of the leading powers in the world, becoming [World Champions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_polo_at_the_World_Aquatics_Championships#Women's_tournament) in [2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_polo_at_the_2011_World_Aquatics_Championships_%E2%80%93_Women%27s_tournament). They won gold at the [2005 World League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_FINA_Women%27s_Water_Polo_World_League) and silver at the [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Women%27s_European_Water_Polo_Championship) and [2012 European Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Women%27s_European_Water_Polo_Championship). The [Greece men's national water polo team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greece_men%27s_national_water_polo_team) became the third best water polo team in the world in [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_World_Aquatics_Championships). The domestic top water polo leagues, [Greek Men's Water Polo League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_Ethniki_Water_Polo) and [Greek Women's Water Polo League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_Ethniki_Women%27s_Water_Polo) are considered amongst the top national leagues in European water polo, as its clubs have made significant success in European competitions.&#x20;

The [Greek men's national volleyball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greece_men%27s_national_volleyball_team) has won two bronze medals, one in the [European Volleyball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men%27s_European_Volleyball_Championship) and another one in the [Men's European Volleyball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men%27s_European_Volleyball_League). The Greek league, the [A1 Ethniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_Ethniki_Volleyball), is considered one of the top volleyball leagues in Europe and Greek clubs have had significant success in European competitions. [Olympiacos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiacos_S.C.) is the most successful volleyball club in the country. In handball, [AC Diomidis Argous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AC_Diomidis_Argous) is the only Greek club to have won a [European Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EHF_Challenge_Cup).&#x20;

### Public holidays and festivals

Main article: [Public holidays in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Greece)



Procession in honour of the Assumption of [Virgin Mary](73513.md) (15 August), a major holiday

According to Greek law, every Sunday of the year is a public holiday. Since the late '70s, Saturday has also been a non-school and non-working day. In addition, there are four mandatory official public holidays: 25 March (*[Greek Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Independence_Day)*), [Easter Monday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Monday), 15 August (*[Assumption or Dormition of the Holy Virgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dormition_of_the_Mother_of_God)*), and 25 December (*Christmas*). 1 May (*Labour Day*) and 28 October (*[Ohi Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohi_Day)*) are regulated by law as being optional but it is customary for employees to be given the day off. There are, however, more public holidays celebrated in Greece than announced by the Ministry of Labour each year as either obligatory or optional, giving a total of eleven national holidays each year. In addition to the national holidays, there are public holidays that are celebrated only by a specific professional group or a local community. For example, many municipalities have a "Patron Saint" parallel to "[Name Days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_days_in_Greece)", or a "Liberation Day".[^cite_note-431] Notable festivals, beyond the religious feasts, include [Patras Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patras_Carnival), [Athens Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athens_Festival), and local wine festivals. The city of [Thessaloniki](40471.md) is also home of a number of festivals and events. The [Thessaloniki International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Thessaloniki_Film_Festival) is one of the most important film festivals in Southern Europe.[^cite_note-432]&#x20;

They formed an alliance, which we call the Hellenic League, and bound themselves not just to repel the Persians, but to help one another whatever particular enemy threatened the freedom of the Greek cities. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-226-81316-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-226-81316-9). The Klephtic ballads developed in mainland Greece, and in the eighteenth century represented the final and supreme stage in the evolution of modern Greek folk poetry. They can be separated into two groups, one dealing with the achievements or the death of an individual Klepht and the second with the life of the Klephts in general...Among them is a notable series of songs that deal with battles between mountains, a motif that goes back to antiquity, as we know from a fragment of Corinna found on a second-century papyrus in which Mount Helicon and Mount Cithaeron are fighting. Such battles of mountains in Greek folk songs have survived in Crete and in Karpathos, but the most famous of all is the Pan-Hellenic ballad of *The Battle between Mount Olympus and Mount Kisavos*.
